=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given three integers n, x, and y.
An event is being held for n performers. When a performer arrives, they are assigned to one of the x stages. All performers assigned to the same stage will perform together as a band, though some stages might remain empty.
After all performances are completed, the jury will award each band a score in the range [1, y].
Return the total number of possible ways the event can take place.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
Note that two events are considered to have been held differently if either of the following conditions is satisfied:

Any performer is assigned a different stage.
Any band is awarded a different score.

 
Example 1:

Input: n = 1, x = 2, y = 3
Output: 6
Explanation:

There are 2 ways to assign a stage to the performer.
The jury can award a score of either 1, 2, or 3 to the only band.


Example 2:

Input: n = 5, x = 2, y = 1
Output: 32
Explanation:

Each performer will be assigned either stage 1 or stage 2.
All bands will be awarded a score of 1.


Example 3:

Input: n = 3, x = 3, y = 4
Output: 684

 
Constraints:

1 <= n, x, y <= 1000

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def numberOfWays(self, n: int, x: int, y: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Then F(x)=∑_{j=0}^x j^n ∑_{k=j}^x C(x,k) C(k,j) y^k (-1)^{k-j}. C(x,k)C(k,j)=C(x,j)C(x-j,k-j). Let l=k-j. Sum = C(x,j) y^j ∑_{l=0}^{x-j} C(x-j,l)(-y)^l = C(x,j)y^j(1-y)^{x-j}. Yes. Good.

Let's maybe implement using Stirling DP instead because formula with (1-y) may be surprising but code simpler. Need ensure no issue with negative base in pow with mod. Python pow negative base with mod is okay but to be safe use ((1 - y) % MOD). Let's write:
MOD = 10**9 + 7
class Solution:
    def numberOfWays(self, n: int, x: int, y: int) -> int:
        fact = [1]*(x+1)
        for i in range(1,x+1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i % MOD
        invfact = [1]*(x+1)
        invfact[x] = pow(fact[x], MOD-2, MOD)
        for i in range(x,0,-1):
            invfact[i-1] = invfact[i]*i % MOD
        base = (1 - y) % MOD
        ans = 0
        for t in range(x+1):
            comb = fact[x] * invfact[t] % MOD * invfact[x-t] % MOD
            term = comb
            term = term * pow(t, n, MOD) % MOD
            term = term * pow(y, t, MOD) % MOD
            term = term * pow(base, x-t, MOD) % MOD
            ans = (ans + term) % MOD
        return ans

MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def numberOfWays(self, n: int, x: int, y: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        fact = [1] * (x + 1)
        for i in range(1, x + 1):
            fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD
        inv_fact = [1] * (x + 1)
        inv_fact[x] = pow(fact[x], MOD - 2, MOD)
        for i in range(x, 0, -1):
            inv_fact[i-1] = inv_fact[i] * i % MOD

        base = (1 - y) % MOD
        ans = 0
        for t in range(x + 1):
            comb = fact[x] * inv_fact[t] % MOD * inv_fact[x - t] % MOD
            term = comb * pow(t, n, MOD) % MOD
            term = term * pow(y, t, MOD) % MOD
            term = term * pow(base, x - t, MOD) % MOD
            ans = (ans + term) % MOD
        return ans

Now, let's perhaps think if there is an alternative formula using "Stirling numbers of the second kind" that might be more expected and maybe code with DP could be easier to verify. Let's compare code lengths:
Stirling DP:
```
MOD=10**9+7
class Solution:
    def numberOfWays(self,n,x,y):
        MOD=10**9+7
        limit=min(n,x)
        stir=[0]*(limit+1)
        stir[0]=1
        for i in range(1,n+1):
            upper=min(i,limit)
            for j in range(upper,0,-1):
                stir[j]=(stir[j-1]+j*stir[j])%MOD
            stir[0]=0
        ans=0
        fall=1
        ypow=1
        for k in range(1,limit+1):
            fall=fall*(x-k+1)%MOD
            ypow=ypow*y%MOD
            ans=(ans+fall*stir[k]%MOD*ypow)%MOD
        return ans
```
This is also concise and maybe more directly tied to original. Need check 1D Stirling recurrence carefully. Let's test n=3,x=3,y=4:
limit=3, stir init [1,0,0,0]
i=1 upper1: j=1 stir[1]=stir[0]+1*stir[1]=1; stir[0]=0 =>[0,1,0,0]
i=2 upper2: j=2 stir[2]=stir[1]+2*stir[2]=1; j=1 stir[1]=stir[0]+1*stir[1]=1; stir[0]=0 =>[0,1,1,0]
i=3 upper3: j=3 stir[3]=stir[2]+3*stir[3]=1; j=2 stir[2]=stir[1]+2*stir[2]=1+2=3; j=1 stir[1]=stir[0]+1*stir[1]=1; =>[0,1,3,1]
ans: k=1 fall=3 ypow=4 ans=12; k=2 fall=3*2=6 ypow=16 ans=12+6*3*16=300? Wait 6*3*16=288, ans=300? Actually 12+288=300, not 684? Then k=3 fall=6*1=6 ypow=64 ans=300+6*1*64=684. Good. This DP code is also correct. It avoids formula negative base and factorial inverses. Complexity O(n*limit) =1e6, fine. Which code should final use? Both accepted. The formula code is shorter? Let's compare: formula code with factorial inverses ~25 lines; Stirling DP ~20 lines. Stirling DP maybe more intuitive and less risk of formula sign? But 1D Stirling recurrence has subtle dp[0]=0; need ensure correct. Formula code maybe more mathematically elegant but factorial inverses. Both okay.

- Need `limit = min(n, x)`. If n=1000,x=1000, limit=1000.

Which code is less likely to have off-by-one? Stirling DP answer uses `fall` and `ypow`:
- `fall` initially 1. For k=1, fall = fall*(x-1+1)=x. Good.

Now, maybe there is a direct DP that computes answer without Stirling:
Let dp[j] = number of ways for processed performers with j nonempty stages and scores? Actually scores can be assigned at end. We could compute sum over assignments of y^{nonempty} by DP:
dp[j] after processing some performers? When adding a performer to an existing nonempty stage, score count unchanged; to an empty stage, number of nonempty stages increases and score choices multiply by y? Need track number of ways including scores? Could compute directly:
Initialize dp[0]=1? For each performer, assign to one of x stages. If assigned to one of j existing nonempty stages: j choices, score factor unchanged. If assigned to one of x-j empty stages: (x-j) choices, new band gets y score choices, multiply by y. Then after n performers, answer = sum_j dp[j]? Let's derive:
Let dp[j] = total weighted count over assignments of processed performers with exactly j nonempty stages, where weight = y^{j}? Wait when a new stage becomes nonempty, we need multiply by y for its score. Existing stage scores already counted. So recurrence:
new[j] += dp[j] * j (assign to existing nonempty stage)
new[j+1] += dp[j] * (x-j) * y (assign to empty stage, choose score for new band)
After n performers, answer = sum_j dp[j]? Since weight y^{#nonempty} included. This is an even simpler DP O(n*x)! Let's test:

n=2,x=2,y=2: dp0=1. i=1: dp1=2*2=4? Wait dp1= x*y=4. This represents assignments after 1 performer: 2 stages *3? y=2 =>4. i=2: from dp1=4: assign to existing stage: j=1, new[1]+=4*1=4; assign to empty stage: (x-j)=1, new[2]+=4*1*y=8. ans=12. Original 12. Good.
n=3,x=3,y=4: Let's compute:
init dp[0]=1.

We process performers one by one. At any point, we have assigned some performers to stages. Some stages nonempty. For each nonempty stage, its band's score has already been chosen? In actual event, scores awarded after all performances, but we can choose score when stage first becomes nonempty. Since score choices independent and fixed once stage nonempty. Weight = y^{number of nonempty stages}. When adding a performer:
- If assigned to an already nonempty stage: there are j such stages. No new score. Weight unchanged.
- If assigned to an empty stage: there are x-j empty stages. This stage becomes nonempty and will need a score; choose one of y scores. Weight multiplied by y.
This counts all stage assignments and score assignments. After all n performers, every nonempty stage has a score, empty stages no score. Sum over j dp[j] gives total weighted count. This is a very direct DP! Complexity O(n*x) with x<=1000. It might be the most intuitive and avoids Stirling and formula. Let's analyze if dp[j] counts assignments of labeled performers? Processing performers one by one in a fixed order (performer 1,2,...,n) and assigning stage choices. Since performers are labeled, this counts all functions. Good.

This DP is elegant and simpler than Stirling? Let's formalize:
`dp[j]` = total number of ways for the first i performers, with exactly j nonempty stages, including score choices for those j stages.
Transition for next performer:
```
ndp = [0]*(limit+1)
for j in range(0, min(i, x)+1):
    if dp[j]==0: continue
    # assign to one of j occupied stages
    ndp[j] = (ndp[j] + dp[j] * j) % MOD
    # assign to one of x-j empty stages; new band score y choices
    if j < x:
        ndp[j+1] = (ndp[j+1] + dp[j] * (x-j) * y) % MOD
dp = ndp
```
After n performers, answer = sum(dp) % MOD. Need limit = min(n,x) because cannot have more nonempty stages than performers or stages. Actually after i performers, j<=min(i,x). We can allocate length min(n,x)+1. But transition uses x-j; if j=limit=x, no empty. If limit=min(n,x) and n<x, after i=n, j<=n; x-j positive for j<limit maybe. Good.

n=3,x=3,y=4: limit=3. We computed sum684. Good.
n=5,x=2,y=1: limit=2. Good.

Base: 0 performers, 0 nonempty stages, weight 1.
Transition: For each partial assignment with j nonempty stages and weight y^j, assigning to occupied stage yields j choices and weight y^j; assigning to empty stage yields x-j choices and weight y^{j+1}. Sum. Good.

Let's write final code:
```python
class Solution:
    def numberOfWays(self, n: int, x: int, y: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        limit = min(n, x)
        dp = [0] * (limit + 1)
        dp[0] = 1

        for _ in range(n):
            ndp = [0] * (limit + 1)
            for j, val in enumerate(dp):
                if val == 0:
                    continue
                # assign to an already non-empty stage
                ndp[j] = (ndp[j] + val * j) % MOD
                # assign to an empty stage; choose score for the new band
                if j + 1 <= limit:
                    ndp[j + 1] = (ndp[j + 1] + val * (x - j) * y) % MOD
            dp = ndp

        return sum(dp) % MOD
```

Now, let's decide final code. I think DP is the best. It is simple and directly models the process. Let's maybe include comments in code to explain. The final answer code block:
```python
class Solution:
    def numberOfWays(self, n: int, x: int, y: int) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        limit = min(n, x)
        dp = [0] * (limit + 1)
        dp[0] = 1

        for _ in range(n):
            ndp = [0] * (limit + 1)
            for j, val in enumerate(dp):
                if val == 0:
                    continue
                # Put this performer on an already occupied stage.
                ndp[j] = (ndp[j] + val * j) % MOD
                # Put this performer on an empty stage; choose a score for the new band.
                if j + 1 <= limit:
                    ndp[j + 1] = (ndp[j + 1] + val * (x - j) * y) % MOD
            dp = ndp

        return sum(dp) % MOD
```
Let's check if `val * (x - j) * y` should include y even if y is score choices for the new band. Yes. But wait: If a stage is empty and we assign a performer, it becomes a band. The score for that band is awarded after all performances. In our DP, we multiply by y at the moment the first performer is assigned to that stage. This counts score choices. Good.

But if y=1000, product val*(x-j)*y ~1e9*1000*1000=1e15, Python okay. If y were 1e9+8, product ~1e27, still Python okay but maybe slower. Constraints no. Use y directly. Good.

```
for i in range(1, n+1):
    ndp = [0]*(limit+1)
    upper = min(i-1, limit)
    for j in range(upper+1):
        val = dp[j]
        if val:
            ndp[j] = (ndp[j] + val * j) % MOD
            if j < limit:
                ndp[j+1] = (ndp[j+1] + val * (x-j) * y) % MOD
    dp = ndp
```
